STAGE-25 → PHASE-05 → STEP-01
Capability Platform Foundation

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-01 establishes the Capability Platform Foundation.

The Capability Platform is the institutional layer that sits between the Provider Platform and all future operational experiences.

It is responsible for governing what COREI can do, independently of:

UI libraries
External systems
Terminal implementations
Business workflows

This creates a deterministic capability abstraction that future platforms consume.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform (Future)
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform   ← STEP-01
        │
        ▼
Workspace Platform
        │
        ▼
Integration Platform
        │
        ▼
Experience Platform

The Capability Platform consumes Providers.

Experiences consume Capabilities.

Experiences never consume Providers directly.

3. Purpose

The Capability Platform answers one institutional question:

"What operational capabilities does COREI provide?"

Instead of exposing vendors such as:

Monaco
xterm.js
AG Grid
Apache ECharts

the platform exposes institutional capabilities such as:

Editor
Terminal
Chart
Grid
Workflow

This creates complete decoupling between operational logic and implementation technologies.

4. Repository Structure

The Capability Platform foundation has been established under:

src/capabilities/

Current structure:

src/capabilities/

contracts/
models/
registry/
manager/
runtime/

state/
dependency/
lifecycle/

ui/

index.ts

The structure follows the same institutional repository pattern introduced by the Architecture Platform and Provider Platform.

5. Core Foundation Components
Capability Contract

Defines the institutional contract that every capability must satisfy.

Responsibilities:

identity
version
enablement
institutional ownership

This becomes the root abstraction for every capability in COREI.

Capability Model

The model represents the runtime definition of a capability.

It extends the capability contract while remaining implementation-independent.

The model becomes the canonical representation stored inside the registry.

Capability Registry

Purpose:

Maintain the authoritative list of registered capabilities.

Responsibilities:

registration
discovery
lookup
institutional ownership

The registry becomes the single source of truth for available capabilities.

Capability Manager

Purpose:

Manage lifecycle-independent operations on capabilities.

Responsibilities include:

register capability
enumerate capabilities
locate capability
expose registry access

The manager isolates operational logic from direct registry manipulation.

Capability Runtime

Defines the runtime state of the Capability Platform itself.

Current responsibility:

initialization state

Future phases will extend runtime governance without modifying its architectural role.

6. Supporting Capability Domains

Although STEP-01 primarily establishes the foundation, the repository has already been prepared for the next capability layers.

These include:

Lifecycle

Future responsibility:

registration
initialization
activation
suspension
recovery
shutdown
State

Future responsibility:

capability state
health
readiness
availability
operational status
Dependency

Future responsibility:

dependency graph
dependency resolution
capability requirements
capability relationships
UI Capability Namespace

Prepared capability families include:

Editor

Terminal

Chart

Grid

Workflow

These are institutional capability namespaces—not implementations.

They will later consume certified UI Providers from Phase-04.

7. Dependency Rules

The Capability Platform introduces the following dependency rules.

Allowed:

Capability
        ↓
Provider

Forbidden:

Capability
        ↓
Vendor

Allowed:

Experience
        ↓
Capability

Forbidden:

Experience
        ↓
Provider

Allowed:

Provider
        ↓
Implementation

These dependency rules become permanent architectural laws.

8. Institutional Benefits

The Capability Platform provides:

vendor independence
implementation abstraction
reusable operational building blocks
deterministic capability discovery
centralized capability governance
future scalability

Future experiences can reuse capabilities without knowing which technology implements them.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-05 → STEP-02
Capability Lifecycle Platform

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-02 establishes the Capability Lifecycle Platform.

While STEP-01 defined what a capability is, STEP-02 defines how a capability behaves throughout its operational existence.

This introduces a standardized lifecycle contract that every capability within COREI must follow, ensuring deterministic initialization, activation, suspension, recovery, and shutdown across the platform.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ├── Capability Foundation      (Step-01)
        └── Capability Lifecycle       (Step-02)

The lifecycle becomes part of the Capability Platform itself and is inherited by future capability families.

3. Purpose

The Capability Lifecycle defines the institutional operational behavior of every capability.

Rather than allowing each capability to implement its own lifecycle independently, COREI establishes a single standardized lifecycle contract that guarantees consistent behavior throughout the platform.

Every capability will operate under the same lifecycle model regardless of its functional purpose.

4. Lifecycle Responsibilities

The lifecycle contract introduces five fundamental operational states.

Initialization

Purpose:

Prepare the capability for operation.

Responsibilities include:

internal setup
dependency preparation
runtime initialization
readiness verification
Activation

Purpose:

Transition the capability into an operational state.

Responsibilities include:

accepting requests
enabling execution
exposing operational services
Suspension

Purpose:

Temporarily pause capability execution while preserving operational state.

Typical scenarios include:

maintenance
dependency interruption
resource optimization
administrative pause
Recovery

Purpose:

Restore a suspended or degraded capability back to a healthy operational state.

Typical responsibilities:

dependency restoration
state reconstruction
runtime verification
operational resumption
Shutdown

Purpose:

Terminate the capability in a controlled and deterministic manner.

Responsibilities include:

releasing resources
unregistering runtime components
preserving required state
graceful termination
5. Default Lifecycle Implementation

A default institutional lifecycle implementation has been established.

Current implementation provides a standardized operational template for all future capability implementations.

Future capability families will inherit this implementation while extending behavior specific to their operational domain.

6. Repository Structure

The lifecycle platform has been organized under:

src/capabilities/lifecycle/

Current contents:

capability-lifecycle.ts

default-capability-lifecycle.ts

index.ts

Responsibilities are clearly separated:

lifecycle contract
default implementation
public API
7. Public API Consolidation

During STEP-02, the Capability Platform public API was expanded to expose all institutional namespaces.

Current public modules include:

Contracts

Models

Registry

Manager

Runtime

Lifecycle

State

Dependency

UI Capability Families

This establishes a single entry point for the Capability Platform.

8. Lifecycle Governance

The Capability Lifecycle introduces several institutional governance principles.

Every capability must:

implement the institutional lifecycle contract
support deterministic initialization
support controlled activation
support graceful suspension
support deterministic recovery
support controlled shutdown

Capabilities are no longer simple runtime objects; they become governed operational entities.

9. Architectural Benefits

The lifecycle platform provides:

standardized operational behavior
predictable capability execution
deterministic runtime transitions
unified lifecycle governance
simplified future capability implementation
consistent operational management across COREI

This prevents individual capability implementations from introducing inconsistent runtime behavior.

Validation completed successfully.

Results:

No circular dependencies detected.
Lifecycle contract implemented.
Default lifecycle implementation completed.
Lifecycle public API established.
Capability Platform public API updated.

contracts/

state/

ui/

index.ts

The lifecycle follows the established COREI dependency model:

        │

Capability Platform
        │

Capability Families

STAGE-25 → PHASE-05 → STEP-02

STAGE-25 → PHASE-05 → STEP-03
Capability State Platform

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-03 establishes the Capability State Platform.

While STEP-01 defined the structural foundation of capabilities and STEP-02 established their operational lifecycle, STEP-03 introduces the institutional runtime state model that governs the operational condition of every capability.

The Capability State Platform becomes the authoritative representation of a capability's current operational state throughout the COREI Operating System.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation            (Step-01)
        ├── Lifecycle             (Step-02)
        └── State                 (Step-03)

The state platform complements the lifecycle by providing continuous visibility into the operational condition of every capability.

3. Purpose

The Capability State Platform answers the institutional question:

"What is the current operational condition of this capability?"

Rather than relying on arbitrary boolean flags or implementation-specific variables, COREI establishes a standardized runtime state model that every capability must expose.

This enables deterministic monitoring, orchestration, governance, and operational decision-making across the platform.

4. Capability State Model

The Capability State aggregates four independent operational dimensions.

CapabilityState

├── Status
├── Health
├── Readiness
└── Availability

Each dimension represents a different aspect of operational behavior.

5. Capability Status

Capability Status represents the operational lifecycle position of a capability.

Supported states:

REGISTERED

INITIALIZED

ACTIVE

SUSPENDED

RECOVERING

STOPPED

Purpose:

lifecycle tracking
execution state
runtime orchestration
operational transitions

Status is directly influenced by the Capability Lifecycle introduced in STEP-02.

6. Capability Health

Health represents the functional quality of a capability.

Supported states:

UNKNOWN

HEALTHY

DEGRADED

FAILED

Purpose:

runtime diagnostics
health monitoring
operational confidence
fault reporting

Health does not indicate whether a capability is active; it indicates how well it is functioning.

7. Capability Readiness

Readiness determines whether a capability is prepared to begin execution.

Supported states:

NOT_READY

READY

Typical readiness checks include:

dependency satisfaction
initialization completion
configuration validation
runtime preparation

A capability may be initialized but still not be ready if required dependencies are unavailable.

8. Capability Availability

Availability represents whether the capability is accessible to the platform.

Supported states:

ONLINE

OFFLINE

Purpose:

service availability
runtime accessibility
platform routing
execution eligibility

Availability is independent of health and status.

A capability may be online but degraded, or offline while retaining its previous state.

9. Institutional State Separation

The Capability State Platform deliberately separates four operational concerns.

Dimension	Responsibility
Status	Current lifecycle position
Health	Operational quality
Readiness	Ability to begin execution
Availability	Platform accessibility

This separation prevents multiple operational concepts from being conflated into a single state indicator.

10. Architectural Benefits

The Capability State Platform provides:

deterministic runtime visibility
standardized operational reporting
consistent monitoring interfaces
independent state dimensions
simplified orchestration
reusable governance model

Every future capability will expose the same state interface regardless of its implementation.

11. Repository Structure

The Capability State Platform has been organized under:

src/capabilities/state/

Current contents:

capability-state.ts

capability-status.ts

capability-health.ts

capability-readiness.ts

capability-availability.ts

index.ts

Responsibilities are separated into individual state definitions with a consolidated public API.

12. Runtime Relationship

The interaction between lifecycle and state follows the institutional execution model.

Capability Lifecycle
        │
        ▼
State Transition
        │
        ▼
Capability State
        │
        ├── Status
        ├── Health
        ├── Readiness
        └── Availability

The lifecycle performs transitions, while the state platform records and exposes the current operational condition.

13. Dependency Rules

The Capability State Platform follows the institutional dependency hierarchy.

Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        └── State

The state platform does not depend on:

UI Providers
Foundation Frameworks
External Integrations
Experience Platform

It remains an internal governance component of the Capability Platform.

Capability State

Capability Status

Capability Health

16. Institutional Outcome

With STEP-03 complete, the Capability Platform now possesses three fundamental governance layers:

Capability Foundation

↓

Capability Lifecycle

↓

Capability State

Together they define:

What a capability is.
How a capability operates.
What condition a capability is currently in.

This forms the operational governance core of the Capability Platform and provides a deterministic state model that future dependency management, capability families, workspace composition, and experience orchestration will build upon.

17. Completion Status

STAGE-25 → PHASE-05 → STEP-03

The Capability State Platform is now institutionally established and validated. It provides a standardized operational state model that every capability within COREI will expose, enabling consistent monitoring, lifecycle coordination, orchestration, and governance across the entire operating system.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-05 → STEP-04
Capability Dependency Platform

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-04 establishes the Capability Dependency Platform.

While the previous steps defined:

STEP-01 — What a capability is.
STEP-02 — How a capability behaves.
STEP-03 — What operational state a capability is currently in.

STEP-04 defines:

What a capability depends on before it can operate.

The Capability Dependency Platform introduces deterministic dependency governance across the COREI Operating System.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        └── Dependency

The Dependency Platform becomes one of the core governance layers of the Capability Platform.

3. Purpose

Institutional platforms cannot rely on implicit dependencies.

Every capability must explicitly declare:

what providers it requires
what optional providers it can use
what capabilities it depends upon
how dependencies are resolved
how dependency relationships are represented

This eliminates hidden runtime coupling and enables deterministic platform initialization.

4. Capability Dependency Model

The Capability Dependency model represents direct capability dependencies.

Responsibilities include:

capability identification
dependency declaration
dependency ownership

Example:

Chart Capability

depends on

Apache ECharts Provider

Another example:

Editor Capability

depends on

Monaco Provider

The dependency model becomes the canonical declaration of runtime dependencies.

5. Capability Requirement

The Capability Requirement model separates required dependencies from optional dependencies.

Current structure supports:

Required Providers

Optional Providers

Examples:

Required:

Editor Capability

↓

Monaco Provider

Optional:

Markdown Preview

↓

Mermaid Provider

This separation provides flexibility while maintaining deterministic initialization.

6. Capability Relationship

Capability relationships describe interactions between capabilities.

Unlike dependencies, relationships express structural associations rather than startup requirements.

Examples include:

Workspace Capability

↓

Panel Capability
Chart Capability

↓

Grid Capability

These relationships become important for future workspace composition and orchestration.

7. Dependency Graph

The Capability Dependency Graph is the institutional repository of all capability dependencies.

Responsibilities include:

dependency storage
dependency lookup
graph construction
future dependency validation

The graph becomes the authoritative representation of capability dependency topology.

8. Dependency Resolver

The Capability Dependency Resolver provides deterministic dependency resolution.

Current responsibilities include:

enumerate dependencies
locate dependencies
resolve capability dependency definitions

Future phases will extend this component with:

recursive dependency resolution
dependency ordering
circular dependency detection
startup sequencing
runtime verification

without changing its architectural responsibility.

9. Repository Organization

The Dependency Platform has been organized under:

src/capabilities/dependency/

Current contents:

capability-dependency.ts

capability-requirement.ts

capability-relationship.ts

capability-dependency-graph.ts

capability-dependency-resolver.ts

index.ts

Each file has a single institutional responsibility.

10. Dependency Governance

The Capability Dependency Platform introduces explicit governance rules.

Every capability must declare its dependencies through the Dependency Platform.

Hidden runtime dependencies are prohibited.

Dependency information must remain centralized and discoverable.

Runtime dependency resolution must occur through the institutional resolver rather than ad hoc implementation logic.

11. Dependency Hierarchy

The Capability Dependency Platform enforces the following dependency direction.

Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Dependency
        │
        ▼
Capability Families

Future capabilities consume Providers through declared dependencies.

Capabilities do not bypass the dependency platform.

12. Architectural Benefits

The Dependency Platform provides:

explicit dependency declaration
deterministic initialization
centralized dependency management
future dependency validation
startup orchestration
scalable capability composition

It removes hidden implementation coupling and establishes a single institutional mechanism for dependency governance.

13. Future Extension

The current implementation establishes the dependency foundation.

Future enhancements can be introduced without changing the repository structure.

Examples include:

dependency graph traversal
dependency health propagation
dependency impact analysis
dependency visualization
startup ordering
dependency diagnostics
runtime dependency auditing

The architecture is intentionally extensible while preserving its single responsibility.

Capability Foundation
        │

STAGE-25 → PHASE-05 → STEP-05
UI Capability Platform

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-05 establishes the UI Capability Platform, the first institutional capability family within the Capability Platform.

While the previous steps built the generic governance layers (Foundation, Lifecycle, State, and Dependency), STEP-05 introduces the first domain-specific capability family responsible for user interaction and visual operations.

The UI Capability Platform provides a standardized abstraction for all user interface capabilities while remaining independent of the underlying UI providers.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Dependency
        └── UI Capability Platform

The UI Capability Platform is the first specialization built upon the generic Capability Platform.

3. Purpose

The UI Capability Platform answers the institutional question:

"What user interface capabilities does COREI provide?"

Rather than exposing implementation technologies such as:

Monaco
AG Grid
Apache ECharts
xterm.js
React Flow

COREI exposes institutional UI capabilities such as:

Editor
Terminal
Grid
Chart
Workflow

This maintains complete separation between operational capability and implementation technology.

4. UI Capability Platform Components

The platform introduces five core governance components.

UI Capability Contract

Defines the institutional contract that every UI capability must satisfy.

Responsibilities include:

capability identity
capability name
associated provider
operational enablement

The contract establishes a consistent interface for all UI capability implementations.

UI Capability Model

The UI Capability Model represents the runtime definition of a UI capability.

It extends the UI Capability Contract while remaining independent of any implementation technology.

The model becomes the canonical runtime representation managed by the registry.

UI Capability Registry

The registry provides the authoritative repository for all UI capabilities.

Responsibilities include:

capability registration
capability discovery
capability enumeration
centralized ownership

The registry becomes the single source of truth for UI capability availability.

UI Capability Manager

The manager governs operational interaction with the registry.

Responsibilities include:

registering capabilities
retrieving registered capabilities
managing registry access

Operational logic remains isolated from registry storage.

UI Capability Runtime

Defines the runtime state of the UI Capability Platform.

Current responsibility:

initialization status

Future phases will extend runtime behavior while preserving architectural boundaries.

5. UI Capability Families

The repository now contains the foundational namespaces for the first institutional UI capability families.

Prepared capability families include:

Editor

Terminal

Grid

Chart

Workflow

These are currently organizational namespaces.

Business implementation will occur in subsequent phases.

6. Relationship with Provider Platform

The UI Capability Platform consumes the certified Provider Platform established during Stage-25 Phase-04.

Dependency direction:

UI Capability

        │

        ▼

Provider Platform

        │

        ▼

Runtime Provider

Examples:

Editor Capability

↓

Monaco Provider
Terminal Capability

↓

xterm.js Provider
Grid Capability

↓

AG Grid Provider
Chart Capability

↓

Apache ECharts Provider
Workflow Capability

↓

React Flow Provider

The UI Capability Platform never exposes provider implementations directly.

7. Architectural Separation

The platform enforces strict institutional separation.

Capabilities represent what COREI can do.

Providers represent how COREI performs those capabilities.

Experiences will later consume capabilities rather than providers.

This separation guarantees long-term replaceability and vendor independence.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-05 → STEP-06
Workspace Capability Platform

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-06 establishes the Workspace Capability Platform, the second institutional capability family within the COREI Capability Platform.

While STEP-05 introduced UI capabilities, STEP-06 introduces the capabilities responsible for workspace composition, layout governance, and operator interaction.

It provides the abstraction layer between the generic Capability Platform and the future Workspace Platform implemented in Stage-25 Phase-06.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Dependency
        ├── UI Capability Platform
        └── Workspace Capability Platform

The Workspace Capability Platform extends the Capability Platform by introducing workspace-specific operational capabilities while remaining independent of workspace implementation.

3. Purpose

The Workspace Capability Platform answers the institutional question:

"What workspace-related operational capabilities does COREI provide?"

It defines the abstraction for workspace behavior without introducing implementation details.

Future Workspace Platform components will consume these capabilities rather than implementing workspace behavior directly.

4. Workspace Capability Platform Components

The platform introduces five institutional governance components.

Workspace Capability Contract

Defines the institutional contract that every workspace capability must satisfy.

Responsibilities include:

capability identity
capability name
operational enablement

The contract establishes a consistent interface for every workspace capability.

Workspace Capability Model

Represents the runtime definition of a workspace capability.

The model extends the Workspace Capability Contract and serves as the canonical runtime representation stored within the registry.

Workspace Capability Registry

Provides the authoritative repository for workspace capabilities.

Responsibilities include:

registration
discovery
enumeration
centralized governance

The registry becomes the single source of truth for available workspace capabilities.

Workspace Capability Manager

Provides operational access to the registry.

Responsibilities include:

capability registration
capability retrieval
registry management

Operational logic remains isolated from storage concerns.

Workspace Capability Runtime

Represents the runtime state of the Workspace Capability Platform.

Current responsibility:

initialization status

Future phases will extend runtime behavior while preserving architectural boundaries.

5. Institutional Responsibility

The Workspace Capability Platform defines what workspace operations are possible, not how workspaces are implemented.

Examples of future workspace capabilities include:

Workspace Creation

Workspace Loading

Workspace Persistence

Workspace Switching

Workspace Templates

Workspace Layout

Workspace Panels

Workspace Sessions

Workspace Recovery

These remain capability definitions.

Actual workspace orchestration will be implemented in Stage-25 Phase-06.

6. Relationship with the Workspace Platform

The Workspace Capability Platform is intentionally separated from the Workspace Platform.

Dependency hierarchy:

Workspace Platform
        │
        ▼
Workspace Capabilities
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform

This ensures the Workspace Platform consumes capabilities rather than directly interacting with provider implementations.

7. Relationship with the UI Capability Platform

Workspace capabilities coordinate UI capabilities rather than replacing them.

Example:

Workspace Capability
        │
        ├── Editor Capability
        ├── Chart Capability
        ├── Grid Capability
        ├── Terminal Capability
        └── Workflow Capability

The Workspace Capability Platform composes operational capabilities without introducing implementation coupling.

8. Architectural Separation

The platform maintains strict institutional boundaries.

Workspace Capability Platform owns:

workspace capability contracts
workspace capability models
workspace capability registry
workspace capability manager
workspace capability runtime

It does not own:

workspace layouts
docking implementation
panel rendering
session persistence
navigation
window management

These responsibilities belong to the Workspace Platform in Phase-06.

Very importent Note:

This is exactly the right question to ask before moving into Phase-06.

The answer is yes—but only if we maintain the institutional layering we've been following. Nothing we have built so far is intended to be "documentation code" or scaffolding. Every layer becomes a runtime dependency of the next layer.

Let me zoom completely out to the entire COREI OS.

The complete execution chain
Operator
      │
      ▼
Application
      │
      ▼
Experience
      │
      ▼
Workspace
      │
      ▼
Capabilities
      │
      ▼
Providers
      │
      ▼
Foundation
      │
      ▼
React / Browser

Everything below is consumed by everything above.

Nothing is bypassed.

Example

Suppose the operator opens the Trading Terminal.

The runtime sequence is approximately:

COREI OS

↓

Trading Experience

↓

Trading Workspace

↓

Chart Capability

↓

Visualization Capability

↓

ECharts Provider

↓

Apache ECharts

↓

Browser Canvas

Notice something important.

The Trading Workspace never says:

"Use ECharts."

Instead it says

"I need Chart Capability."

Chart Capability decides

↓

Visualization Capability

↓

Visualization Provider

↓

ECharts

Exactly what institutional software should do.

Another example

Suppose user opens Terminal.

Runtime:

Infrastructure Experience

↓

Infrastructure Workspace

↓

Terminal Capability

↓

UI Capability

↓

Xterm Provider

Why Capability Platform exists

At that point, every provider and capability we've built starts participating in the live runtime.

What happens after Phase-06

Then the progression becomes even more tangible:

Phase-07

↓

Infrastructure Experience

Trading Experience

Research Experience

Risk Experience

Executive Experience

Each experience composes one or more workspaces, which in turn compose capabilities backed by providers.

Why I'm comfortable with the current direction

From reviewing your blueprints, Anchor Law, Master Build Flow, Stage documents, and the long-term vision you've shared, I don't see anything we've built in Phase-04 or Phase-05 as disposable.

They are foundational infrastructure in the same sense that:

the Linux kernel is not visible to the user but every application depends on it,
Kubernetes is rarely visible to an operator but orchestrates the entire platform,
a database driver is never seen directly but every service relies on it.

Phase-04 (Providers) and Phase-05 (Capabilities) play that role inside COREI OS. They are not "demo code"; they are the runtime contracts that every future workspace, experience, and application will consume. That is why we've been careful to keep them vendor-independent and aligned with the institutional architecture.

This is also why I recommended freezing Phase-05 after validation. It gives us a stable platform to build visible functionality in Phase-06 without having to revisit these foundations later. That is the pattern used in long-lived institutional platforms, and it aligns with your goal of building something that remains maintainable for the next 10–15 years.

-------------------

Excellent. This is the point where the architecture starts becoming visible.

Up to now we have built:

Phase-04 → Provider Platform (technology abstraction)
Phase-05 → Capability Platform (business abstraction)

Now we begin building the Workspace Platform, which is the first platform that composes those abstractions into something the operator will eventually see and interact with.

The dependency chain remains:

Foundation
      ↓
Providers
      ↓
Capabilities
      ↓
Workspace Platform   ← We start here
      ↓
Experience Platform
      ↓
Applications

From this point onward, every implementation should consume the Provider and Capability platforms rather than bypassing them.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-06 → STEP-01
Workspace Platform Foundation

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-01 establishes the Workspace Platform Foundation, the first implementation layer of the Workspace Platform within the COREI Operating System.

While Stage-25 Phase-05 introduced the Capability Platform, this step introduces the platform responsible for composing, orchestrating, and managing capabilities into operational workspaces.

The Workspace Platform becomes the first architectural layer that transforms institutional capabilities into operator-facing runtime structures.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Workspace Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Composition
        ├── Panels
        ├── Layout
        ├── Docking
        ├── Persistence
        └── Runtime

The Workspace Platform consumes the Capability Platform and prepares the foundation for future Experience Platforms.

3. Purpose

The Workspace Platform answers the institutional question:

"How are institutional capabilities organized into operator workspaces?"

Capabilities define what COREI can do.

The Workspace Platform defines how those capabilities are assembled into operational environments.

Examples include:

Trading Workspace
Infrastructure Workspace
Risk Workspace
Research Workspace
Executive Workspace
Compliance Workspace

The Workspace Platform does not implement business logic. It orchestrates capability composition.

4. Workspace Platform Components

The Workspace Platform Foundation introduces five institutional governance components.

Workspace Contract

Defines the institutional contract that every workspace must satisfy.

Responsibilities include:

workspace identity
workspace name
capability membership
operational enablement

The contract establishes a consistent interface for all workspace implementations.

Workspace Model

Represents the runtime definition of a workspace.

The model extends the Workspace Contract and serves as the canonical runtime representation stored within the registry.

Workspace Registry

Provides the authoritative repository for all workspace definitions.

Responsibilities include:

workspace registration
workspace discovery
workspace enumeration
centralized governance

The registry becomes the single source of truth for all workspaces.

Workspace Manager

Provides operational interaction with the registry.

Responsibilities include:

registering workspaces
retrieving workspaces
managing registry operations

Operational logic remains isolated from storage concerns.

Workspace Runtime

Defines the runtime state of the Workspace Platform.

Current responsibility:

initialization status

Future phases will extend runtime behavior while preserving architectural stability.

5. Institutional Responsibility

The Workspace Platform Foundation defines what a workspace is, not how it behaves.

Future workspace definitions include:

Trading Workspace

Infrastructure Workspace

Research Workspace

Risk Workspace

Executive Workspace

Compliance Workspace

Development Workspace

Operations Workspace

These are workspace definitions that will later compose capabilities.

6. Relationship with Capability Platform

The Workspace Platform is the first platform to consume the Capability Platform.

Dependency hierarchy:

Workspace Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform

A workspace owns capabilities.

Capabilities own providers.

Providers own implementation technologies.

This hierarchy preserves deterministic architectural separation.

7. Future Runtime Composition

Although STEP-01 establishes only the foundation, the long-term runtime composition is already defined.

Example:

Trading Workspace
        │
        ├── Chart Capability
        ├── Grid Capability
        ├── Terminal Capability
        ├── Editor Capability
        ├── Workflow Capability
        └── Productivity Capability

Each capability internally resolves its required providers.

The Workspace Platform never communicates directly with provider implementations.

8. Architectural Separation

The Workspace Platform Foundation owns:

workspace contracts
workspace models
workspace registry
workspace manager
workspace runtime

It does not own:

panel rendering
docking logic
layout persistence
visualization rendering
provider implementations
business workflows

Those responsibilities are introduced in later steps of Phase-06 or belong to lower platform layers.

9. Repository Organization

The Workspace Platform Foundation has been organized under:

src/workspaces/

Current repository structure:

src/workspaces/

contracts/
    workspace-contract.ts
    index.ts

models/
    workspace.ts
    index.ts

registry/
    workspace-registry.ts
    index.ts

manager/
    workspace-manager.ts
    index.ts

runtime/
    workspace-runtime.ts
    index.ts

index.ts

Each component follows the COREI institutional principle of single responsibility.

10. Dependency Rules

The Workspace Platform follows the approved COREI dependency model.

Allowed:

Experience Platform
        │
        ▼
Workspace Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform

Forbidden:

Workspace Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform

Forbidden:

Workspace Platform
        │
        ▼
Vendor Library

This ensures that workspace implementations remain independent of underlying technologies.

11. Architectural Benefits

The Workspace Platform Foundation provides:

centralized workspace governance
deterministic workspace registration
reusable workspace definitions
capability-based composition
implementation independence
scalable workspace architecture

Future workspace implementations can evolve without modifying the Capability Platform.

12. Validation Results

Repository validation completed successfully.

Validation confirms:

No circular dependencies detected.
Workspace Contract implemented.
Workspace Model implemented.
Workspace Registry implemented.
Workspace Manager implemented.
Workspace Runtime implemented.

Repository organization verified.
13. Repository Status

Current Workspace Platform Foundation structure:

src/workspaces/

contracts/
models/
registry/
manager/
runtime/

index.ts

The Workspace Platform Foundation is now institutionally established.

14. Deliverables

The following institutional components were completed during STEP-01:

Workspace Contract

Workspace Model

Workspace Registry

Workspace Manager

Workspace Runtime

Workspace Platform Public API

These components establish the canonical governance model for all workspace implementations within COREI.

15. Institutional Outcome

With STEP-01 complete, the Workspace Platform now has its foundational governance layer.

The platform is positioned directly above the Capability Platform and will progressively evolve throughout Phase-06.

The current architectural progression is:

Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Workspace Platform Foundation

Subsequent steps in Phase-06 will extend this foundation by introducing:

Workspace Lifecycle
Workspace State
Workspace Composition
Panel Platform
Layout Platform
Docking Platform
Workspace Persistence
Runtime Orchestration

These additions will transform the Workspace Platform from a structural foundation into the runtime environment that assembles institutional capabilities into operator workspaces.

16. Long-Term Architectural Alignment

The Workspace Platform Foundation is fully aligned with COREI's long-term institutional architecture:

Workspace abstraction remains independent of capabilities and providers.
Capabilities are composed rather than reimplemented.
Providers remain hidden behind capability abstractions.
Every workspace follows a common governance model.
New workspaces can be introduced without architectural restructuring.
The platform is designed to scale across all current and future COREI operational environments while preserving deterministic behavior and institutional governance.
17. Completion Status

The Workspace Platform Foundation is now institutionally established and validated. It provides the foundational governance layer for composing capabilities into operational workspaces and marks the transition from architectural abstractions to the runtime structures that will become visible to operators as the remaining Workspace Platform components are implemented throughout Phase-06.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-06 → STEP-02
Workspace Lifecycle Platform

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-02 establishes the Workspace Lifecycle Platform, the operational governance layer responsible for managing the complete lifecycle of every workspace within the COREI Operating System.

While STEP-01 defined the structural foundation of a workspace, STEP-02 defines the standardized operational lifecycle that every workspace must follow throughout its existence.

This ensures that all present and future workspaces behave consistently regardless of their purpose or implementation.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Workspace Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Composition
        ├── Panels
        ├── Layout
        ├── Docking
        ├── Persistence
        └── Runtime

The Workspace Lifecycle Platform extends the Workspace Foundation by introducing operational state transitions while remaining independent of workspace business logic.

3. Purpose

The Workspace Lifecycle Platform answers the institutional question:

"How does a workspace behave throughout its operational lifetime?"

Rather than allowing individual workspaces to define their own lifecycle behavior, COREI establishes a single lifecycle model that every workspace must implement.

This provides deterministic runtime behavior across all operational environments.

4. Workspace Lifecycle Components

The platform introduces two institutional governance components.

Workspace Lifecycle Contract

Defines the standard operational lifecycle interface for every workspace.

Supported lifecycle operations:

Initialize

Activate

Deactivate

Suspend

Resume

Dispose

These operations define the complete lifecycle of a workspace from creation to termination.

Default Workspace Lifecycle

Provides the default implementation of the Workspace Lifecycle contract.

Current implementation establishes:

lifecycle structure
operational interface
future extension point

Business behavior will be introduced in later phases while preserving the contract established in this step.

5. Lifecycle Flow

Every workspace within COREI will follow the same operational sequence.

Workspace Created
        │
        ▼
Initialize
        │
        ▼
Activate
        │
        ▼
Running
        │
        ├── Suspend
        │       │
        │       ▼
        │    Resume
        │
        ▼
Deactivate
        │
        ▼
Dispose

This deterministic flow guarantees that workspace behavior remains consistent throughout the platform.

6. Lifecycle Responsibilities

Each lifecycle stage has a distinct institutional responsibility.

Lifecycle Stage	Responsibility
Initialize	Prepare workspace resources and dependencies
Activate	Make the workspace operational and available to the operator
Deactivate	Gracefully stop active workspace operations
Suspend	Temporarily pause workspace execution while preserving state
Resume	Restore a previously suspended workspace
Dispose	Release workspace resources and terminate execution

Each operation represents a well-defined transition in the workspace lifecycle.

7. Relationship with the Capability Platform

The Workspace Lifecycle Platform does not directly initialize providers or external technologies.

Instead, lifecycle operations will coordinate the activation and deactivation of workspace capabilities.

Runtime dependency flow:

Workspace Lifecycle
        │
        ▼
Workspace
        │
        ▼
Capabilities
        │
        ▼
Providers
        │
        ▼
Foundation

This preserves the institutional dependency hierarchy established in earlier phases.

8. Future Runtime Behavior

Although the lifecycle methods currently provide structural implementations, future phases will extend them with operational behavior such as:

capability initialization
provider activation
layout restoration
workspace persistence recovery
runtime synchronization
event subscriptions
telemetry registration
graceful shutdown

These behaviors will be added without modifying the lifecycle contract.

9. Repository Organization

The Workspace Lifecycle Platform has been organized under:

src/workspaces/lifecycle/

Current repository structure:

workspace-lifecycle.ts

default-workspace-lifecycle.ts

index.ts

Each file has a single institutional responsibility.

10. Architectural Separation

The Workspace Lifecycle Platform owns:

lifecycle contract
default lifecycle implementation
lifecycle public API

It does not own:

workspace state
panel lifecycle
docking behavior
layout persistence
runtime orchestration
business workflows

Those responsibilities belong to later steps within the Workspace Platform.

11. Dependency Rules

The Workspace Lifecycle Platform follows the approved COREI dependency hierarchy.

Allowed:

Workspace Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform

Forbidden:

Workspace Lifecycle
        │
        ▼
Vendor Libraries

Forbidden:

Workspace Lifecycle
        │
        ▼
Direct Provider Implementations

This guarantees implementation independence and long-term maintainability.

12. Architectural Benefits

The Workspace Lifecycle Platform provides:

deterministic workspace behavior
standardized lifecycle governance
reusable lifecycle implementation
consistent activation and shutdown procedures
centralized operational control
scalable lifecycle architecture

Future workspaces inherit a common operational model without redefining lifecycle behavior.

16. Institutional Outcome

Workspace Foundation
        │

Workspace Lifecycle

Together they define:

What a workspace is.
How a workspace behaves throughout its operational lifetime.

This standardized lifecycle ensures that every future workspace—including Trading, Infrastructure, Research, Risk, Executive, Compliance, and AI—follows the same initialization, activation, suspension, resumption, deactivation, and disposal model, providing deterministic runtime behavior across the entire operating system.

17. Long-Term Architectural Alignment

The Workspace Lifecycle Platform aligns with COREI's long-term institutional architecture:

A single lifecycle contract governs every workspace.
Lifecycle behavior is independent of workspace purpose.
Capabilities and providers remain hidden behind workspace abstractions.
Future lifecycle enhancements can be introduced without altering the contract.
The architecture supports long-term scalability, operational consistency, and maintainability across all current and future COREI workspaces.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-06 → STEP-03
Workspace State Platform

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-03 establishes the Workspace State Platform, the institutional governance layer responsible for representing the complete operational state of every workspace within the COREI Operating System.

While STEP-02 defined the lifecycle transitions of a workspace, STEP-03 defines the authoritative runtime state model that describes the current operational condition of a workspace at any point in time.

This state model becomes the single source of truth for all higher Workspace Platform components.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Workspace Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Composition
        ├── Panels
        ├── Layout
        ├── Docking
        ├── Persistence
        └── Runtime

The Workspace State Platform extends the Workspace Lifecycle by introducing deterministic operational state while remaining independent of business functionality.

3. Purpose

The Workspace State Platform answers the institutional question:

"What is the current operational condition of a workspace?"

Rather than allowing individual workspace implementations to manage their own runtime status, COREI establishes a unified state model that every workspace follows.

This provides consistent operational visibility across the entire operating system.

4. Workspace State Components

The Workspace State Platform introduces five institutional governance components.

Workspace Availability

Represents whether a workspace is available for operation.

Supported values:

AVAILABLE

UNAVAILABLE

Availability represents operational accessibility.

Workspace Health

Represents the health condition of a workspace.

Supported values:

HEALTHY

DEGRADED

FAILED

Health enables higher platform layers to evaluate operational reliability.

Workspace Readiness

Represents whether a workspace is prepared for execution.

Supported values:

READY

NOT_READY

Readiness is used by runtime orchestration before activation.

Workspace Status

Represents the lifecycle position of a workspace.

Supported values:

CREATED

INITIALIZED

ACTIVE

SUSPENDED

INACTIVE

DISPOSED

Status reflects the current lifecycle phase defined during STEP-02.

Workspace State

The Workspace State aggregates all operational state information into a single authoritative model.

It includes:

status
health
readiness
availability

This becomes the canonical runtime state representation for every workspace.

5. Relationship with Workspace Lifecycle

The Workspace Lifecycle defines the sequence of operations.

The Workspace State records the current operational outcome of those operations.

Relationship:

Workspace Lifecycle
        │
        ▼
Workspace State
        │
        ▼
Workspace Runtime

Lifecycle controls transitions.

State records operational reality.

6. Relationship with the Capability Platform

The Workspace State Platform does not monitor provider technologies directly.

Instead, it represents the aggregate operational condition of the workspace after evaluating the capabilities it contains.

Runtime flow:

Workspace
        │
        ▼
Capabilities
        │
        ▼
Capability States
        │
        ▼
Workspace State

Workspace state therefore becomes a higher-level operational abstraction built upon capability states.

7. Future Runtime Usage

Future Workspace Platform components will consume the Workspace State.

Examples include:

Layout Platform
        │
        ▼
Only restore READY workspaces.
Docking Platform
        │
        ▼
Prevent rendering FAILED workspaces.
Persistence Platform
        │
        ▼
Persist ACTIVE workspaces.
Runtime Orchestrator
        │
        ▼
Activate AVAILABLE and READY workspaces only.

This establishes deterministic runtime decision-making throughout COREI.

8. Repository Organization

The Workspace State Platform has been organized under:

src/workspaces/state/

Current repository contents:

workspace-health.ts

workspace-readiness.ts

workspace-status.ts

workspace-state.ts

index.ts

Each file follows the COREI institutional principle of single responsibility.

9. Architectural Separation

The Workspace State Platform owns:

workspace availability
workspace health
workspace readiness
workspace status
workspace state model

It does not own:

lifecycle execution
panel rendering
layout composition
docking logic
persistence
runtime orchestration
business workflows

These responsibilities remain in later Workspace Platform layers.

The Workspace State Platform follows the approved COREI dependency hierarchy.

        │
        ▼
Workspace State
        │

Workspace Lifecycle
        │

Capability Platform

Forbidden:

Workspace State
        │

Provider Platform

Forbidden:

Workspace State
        │

Vendor Libraries

This preserves implementation independence and deterministic architecture.

11. Architectural Benefits

centralized workspace state governance
deterministic operational status
unified runtime visibility
reusable state model
standardized health reporting
scalable operational monitoring

Every workspace now exposes a common operational state model.

17. Practical Runtime Example

Once later phases are complete, a Trading Workspace may expose a runtime state such as:

Readiness     : NOT_READY
Availability  : UNAVAILABLE

Every COREI service will consume this standardized model rather than maintaining independent workspace state.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-06 → STEP-04
Workspace Composition Platform

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-04 establishes the Workspace Composition Platform, the first integration layer that transforms the Workspace Platform from a structural framework into an operational orchestration platform.

While the previous steps defined what a workspace is, how it behaves, and what operational state it maintains, STEP-04 defines how capabilities are assembled into a complete workspace.

This is the first point where the Workspace Platform begins consuming the Capability Platform developed during Phase-05.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Workspace Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Composition
        ├── Panels
        ├── Layout
        ├── Docking
        ├── Persistence
        └── Runtime

The Workspace Composition Platform becomes the orchestration layer responsible for binding capabilities into operational workspace structures.

3. Purpose

The Workspace Composition Platform answers the institutional question:

"Which capabilities belong to a workspace, where are they positioned, and how are they organized into a unified operational environment?"

Rather than constructing workspaces from UI components or framework-specific layouts, COREI constructs workspaces from institutional capabilities.

Examples include:

Trading Workspace
Infrastructure Workspace
Risk Workspace
Research Workspace
Executive Workspace
Compliance Workspace

Each workspace is defined as a composition of reusable capabilities.

4. Workspace Composition Components

The Workspace Composition Platform introduces four institutional governance components.

Workspace Area

Defines the logical placement areas available within every workspace.

Supported areas:

TOP

LEFT

CENTER

RIGHT

BOTTOM

These represent logical regions rather than implementation-specific layouts.

Workspace Region

Represents a named region within a workspace.

Responsibilities include:

region identity
assigned workspace area

Regions provide structured placement for capabilities while remaining independent of any docking or rendering technology.

Workspace Capability Binding

Represents the institutional relationship between a workspace region and a capability.

Responsibilities include:

capability identity
target region
enablement status

This binding allows a workspace to consume capabilities without exposing provider implementations.

Workspace Composition

Represents the complete composition of a workspace.

A composition contains:

workspace identity
workspace regions
capability bindings

The composition becomes the authoritative definition of a workspace layout at the architectural level.

5. First Capability Integration

STEP-04 marks the first formal integration between the Workspace Platform and the Capability Platform.

Dependency hierarchy:

Workspace Composition
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform

This preserves the COREI institutional dependency model while allowing workspaces to remain independent of implementation technologies.

6. Institutional Composition Model

A workspace is no longer viewed as a collection of UI elements.

Instead, it is defined as a composition of capabilities.

Example:

Trading Workspace
│
├── TOP
│      └── Productivity Capability
│
├── LEFT
│      └── Workflow Capability
│
├── CENTER
│      ├── Chart Capability
│      └── Grid Capability
│
├── RIGHT
│      └── Editor Capability
│
└── BOTTOM
       └── Terminal Capability

The workspace defines what capabilities exist and where they belong, while the capabilities themselves determine how functionality is implemented.

7. Relationship with the Capability Platform

The Workspace Composition Platform consumes institutional capabilities rather than implementation technologies.

For example:

Chart Capability
        │
        ▼
Visualization Capability
        │
        ▼
ECharts Provider
Terminal Capability
        │
        ▼
UI Capability
        │
        ▼
xterm.js Provider

The composition layer never references providers or external libraries directly.

8. Relationship with Future Workspace Layers

Future Workspace Platform layers will build upon the composition model.

Examples:

Workspace Composition
        │
        ▼
Panel Platform

Each capability binding will later become one or more operational panels.

Workspace Composition
        │
        ▼
Layout Platform

Regions will later receive physical layout definitions.

Workspace Composition
        │
        ▼
Docking Platform

Docking will organize the composed regions visually.

Thus, STEP-04 establishes the architectural foundation upon which visible workspaces will be constructed.

9. Repository Organization

The Workspace Composition Platform has been organized under:

src/workspaces/composition/

Current repository structure:

workspace-area.ts

workspace-region.ts

workspace-capability-binding.ts

workspace-composition.ts

index.ts

Each component follows the COREI institutional principle of single responsibility.

10. Architectural Separation

The Workspace Composition Platform owns:

workspace areas
workspace regions
capability bindings
workspace composition definitions

It does not own:

panel rendering
docking behavior
layout management
persistence
runtime execution
provider implementations
business workflows

These responsibilities are implemented in subsequent Workspace Platform steps.

11. Dependency Rules

The Workspace Composition Platform follows the approved COREI dependency hierarchy.

Allowed:

Workspace Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform

Forbidden:

Workspace Composition
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform

Forbidden:

Workspace Composition
        │
        ▼
Vendor Libraries

This ensures that workspace definitions remain completely technology-independent.

12. Architectural Benefits

The Workspace Composition Platform provides:

capability-based workspace construction
deterministic workspace organization
reusable capability composition
centralized workspace definitions
implementation independence
scalable workspace architecture

New workspaces can be created by composing capabilities without modifying lower architectural layers.

Workspace Capability Binding implemented.
Workspace Composition implemented.

src/workspaces/composition/

workspace-area.ts

workspace-region.ts

workspace-capability-binding.ts

workspace-composition.ts

index.ts

The Workspace Composition Platform is now institutionally established.

15. Deliverables

The following institutional components were completed during STEP-04:

Workspace Area

Workspace Region

Workspace Capability Binding

Workspace Composition

Workspace Composition Public API

These components establish the canonical composition model for all workspaces within COREI.

16. Institutional Outcome

With STEP-04 complete, the Workspace Platform now consists of four governance layers:

Workspace Foundation
        │
        ▼
Workspace Lifecycle
        │
        ▼
Workspace State
        │
        ▼
Workspace Composition

Together they define:

What a workspace is.
How a workspace behaves.
What operational state it maintains.
How institutional capabilities are assembled into that workspace.

This marks the transition from foundational abstractions to executable workspace structures.

17. Practical Runtime Example

A future Infrastructure Workspace may be composed as follows:

Infrastructure Workspace

TOP
    └── Productivity Capability

LEFT
    └── Workflow Capability

CENTER
    ├── Grid Capability
    ├── Visualization Capability
    └── Editor Capability

RIGHT
    └── Monitoring Capability

BOTTOM
    └── Terminal Capability

At runtime:

The Workspace Platform resolves the composition.
The Capability Platform resolves each capability.
The Provider Platform resolves the appropriate technology providers.
The Foundation Platform supplies the underlying runtime.

Each layer remains responsible only for its own institutional role.

18. Long-Term Architectural Alignment

The Workspace Composition Platform aligns with COREI's long-term institutional architecture by ensuring:

Workspaces are composed from capabilities rather than UI technologies.
Capability composition remains independent of providers.
Workspace definitions are reusable across multiple experiences.
New capabilities can be introduced without redesigning existing workspaces.
New workspaces can be assembled through composition instead of duplication.
The architecture scales across all future COREI operational environments while preserving deterministic behavior, vendor independence, and institutional governance.
19. Completion Status

Status: COMPLETED

The Workspace Composition Platform is now institutionally established and validated. It represents the first major integration point between the Workspace Platform and the Capability Platform, providing the canonical model for assembling institutional capabilities into operational workspaces and laying the foundation for the upcoming Panel Platform, Layout Platform, Docking Platform, Persistence Platform, and Workspace Runtime Orchestration.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-06 → STEP-05
Panel Platform

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-05 establishes the Panel Platform, the institutional runtime layer responsible for transforming workspace compositions into operational panel instances.

While STEP-04 defined which capabilities belong to a workspace, STEP-05 defines how those capabilities are represented as runtime panels.

This is the first architectural layer that directly bridges workspace composition with the future visual runtime of the COREI Operating System.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Workspace Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Composition
        ├── Panel Platform
        ├── Layout
        ├── Docking
        ├── Persistence
        └── Runtime

The Panel Platform consumes Workspace Composition and prepares the runtime entities that will later be arranged by the Layout and Docking Platforms.

3. Purpose

The Panel Platform answers the institutional question:

"How does a capability become an operational panel inside a workspace?"

A panel is not a UI component or framework widget.

A panel is an institutional runtime object responsible for hosting exactly one capability within a workspace.

Examples include:

Chart Panel
Grid Panel
Terminal Panel
Editor Panel
Workflow Panel
Dashboard Panel

Each panel acts as the operational container for a capability.

4. Panel Platform Components

The Panel Platform introduces five institutional governance components.

Panel Contract

Defines the institutional contract for every panel.

Responsibilities include:

panel identity
panel title
associated capability
target workspace region
operational enablement

The contract establishes a consistent interface for all panel implementations.

Panel Model

Represents the runtime definition of a panel.

The model extends the Panel Contract and becomes the canonical runtime representation stored within the registry.

Panel Registry

Provides the authoritative repository for all runtime panels.

Responsibilities include:

panel registration
panel discovery
panel enumeration
centralized governance

The registry becomes the single source of truth for all panels.

Panel Manager

Provides operational interaction with the registry.

Responsibilities include:

registering panels
retrieving panels
managing registry operations

Operational logic remains isolated from storage concerns.

Panel Runtime

Defines the runtime state of the Panel Platform.

Current responsibility:

initialization status

Future phases will extend runtime behavior while preserving architectural stability.

5. Relationship with Workspace Composition

The Panel Platform directly consumes the Workspace Composition Platform.

Runtime relationship:

Workspace
        │
        ▼
Workspace Composition
        │
        ▼
Capability Binding
        │
        ▼
Panel

Every capability binding defined during STEP-04 eventually becomes one operational panel.

6. Relationship with the Capability Platform

The Panel Platform never hosts implementation technologies directly.

Instead, every panel hosts exactly one institutional capability.

Runtime hierarchy:

Panel
        │
        ▼
Capability
        │
        ▼
Provider
        │
        ▼
Technology

This preserves the vendor-independent architecture established during Phase-04 and Phase-05.

7. Practical Runtime Model

Future Trading Workspace:

Trading Workspace
│
├── Chart Panel
│      │
│      ▼
│   Chart Capability
│
├── Grid Panel
│      │
│      ▼
│   Grid Capability
│
├── Editor Panel
│      │
│      ▼
│   Editor Capability
│
└── Terminal Panel
       │
       ▼
    Terminal Capability

Each panel is responsible only for hosting its assigned capability.

8. Relationship with Future Workspace Layers

The Panel Platform becomes the input for the remaining Workspace Platform components.

Future dependency chain:

Workspace Composition
        │
        ▼
Panel Platform
        │
        ▼
Layout Platform
        │
        ▼
Docking Platform
        │
        ▼
Workspace Runtime

This establishes a deterministic progression from logical composition to visual runtime.

Each file follows the COREI institutional principle of single responsibility.

10. Architectural Separation

panel runtime

It does not own:

docking behavior
layout algorithms
visualization libraries

business workflows

Those responsibilities belong to later Workspace Platform layers or lower architectural platforms.

12. Architectural Benefits

standardized runtime panel abstraction
centralized panel governance
reusable panel model
deterministic capability hosting
provider independence
scalable panel architecture

New capabilities automatically gain a consistent runtime container without architectural changes.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-06 → STEP-06
Layout Platform

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-06 establishes the Layout Platform, the institutional layer responsible for defining the spatial organization of runtime panels within a workspace.

While STEP-05 introduced the Panel Platform, which defines what is displayed, STEP-06 defines where those panels are organized inside a workspace.

This separation ensures that workspace organization remains independent of any rendering framework, docking library, or browser technology.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Workspace Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Composition
        ├── Panel Platform
        ├── Layout Platform
        ├── Docking Platform
        ├── Persistence
        └── Runtime

The Layout Platform consumes the Panel Platform and prepares panel organization for future Docking and Runtime orchestration.

3. Purpose

The Layout Platform answers the institutional question:

"How are runtime panels organized inside a workspace?"

A layout is not:

CSS Grid
Flexbox
Dockview layout
Browser DOM structure

A layout is an institutional model describing panel organization that can later be rendered by any layout provider.

This abstraction allows COREI to remain independent of rendering technologies.

4. Layout Platform Components

The Layout Platform introduces five institutional governance components.

Layout Contract

Defines the canonical contract for every workspace layout.

Responsibilities include:

layout identity
workspace ownership
panel membership
enablement state

The contract standardizes all layout definitions.

Layout Model

Represents the runtime implementation of a layout.

The model extends the Layout Contract and becomes the canonical runtime representation stored within the registry.

Layout Registry

Provides the authoritative repository for all workspace layouts.

Responsibilities include:

layout registration
layout discovery
layout enumeration
centralized governance

The registry becomes the single source of truth for layout definitions.

Layout Manager

Provides operational interaction with the registry.

Responsibilities include:

registering layouts
retrieving layouts
managing layout collections

Operational logic remains isolated from storage concerns.

Layout Runtime

Defines the runtime state of the Layout Platform.

Current responsibility:

initialization status

Future phases will extend runtime behavior without modifying the platform contract.

5. Relationship with the Panel Platform

The Layout Platform consumes runtime panels produced by the Panel Platform.

Runtime relationship:

Workspace
        │
        ▼
Workspace Composition
        │
        ▼
Panels
        │
        ▼
Layout

The Layout Platform never creates panels.

It only organizes existing runtime panels.

6. Relationship with the Capability Platform

The Layout Platform remains completely independent of capability implementation.

Dependency hierarchy:

Layout
        │
        ▼

        │
        ▼

        │
        ▼
Provider

STAGE-25 → PHASE-06 → STEP-07
Docking Platform

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-07 establishes the Docking Platform, the institutional runtime layer responsible for managing operator interaction with workspace panels.

While the Layout Platform defines where panels belong, the Docking Platform defines how operators interact with those panels during runtime.

This distinction preserves the separation between architectural intent and user interaction, ensuring that workspace composition remains immutable while runtime arrangements remain flexible.

2. Architectural Position
Architecture
        │
        ▼
Foundation Platform
        │
        ▼
Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Capability Platform
        │
        ▼
Workspace Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Composition
        ├── Panel Platform
        ├── Layout Platform
        ├── Docking Platform
        ├── Persistence
        └── Runtime

The Docking Platform sits directly above the Layout Platform and below the future Persistence and Runtime orchestration layers.

3. Purpose

The Docking Platform answers the institutional question:

"How can an operator manipulate workspace panels without changing the architectural definition of the workspace?"

A docking system is not:

Dockview
Golden Layout
FlexLayout
Browser window management

Instead, it is an institutional abstraction governing runtime interaction.

The implementation technology remains replaceable.

4. Docking Platform Components

The Docking Platform introduces six institutional governance components.

Dock Position

Defines the supported docking positions within a workspace.

Supported positions:

LEFT

RIGHT

TOP

BOTTOM

CENTER

FLOATING

These positions represent logical placement semantics independent of any UI framework.

Dock Node Contract

Defines the canonical contract for every dockable runtime node.

Responsibilities include:

dock node identity
hosted panel identity
docking position
visibility state

The contract standardizes all dockable runtime entities.

Dock Node Model

Represents the runtime implementation of a dock node.

The model extends the Dock Node Contract and becomes the canonical runtime representation stored within the registry.

Dock Registry

Provides the authoritative repository for all dock nodes.

Responsibilities include:

dock node registration
dock node discovery
dock node enumeration
centralized governance

The registry becomes the single source of truth for docking state.

Dock Manager

Provides operational interaction with the Dock Registry.

Responsibilities include:

registering dock nodes
retrieving dock nodes
managing docking collections

Operational logic remains isolated from storage concerns.

Dock Runtime

Defines the runtime state of the Docking Platform.

Current responsibility:

initialization status

Future phases will extend runtime behavior to support interactive panel operations while preserving the established contracts.

5. Relationship with the Layout Platform

The Docking Platform consumes layouts but does not create them.

Runtime relationship:

Workspace
        │
        ▼
Workspace Composition
        │
        ▼
Panels
        │
        ▼
Layout
        │
        ▼
Docking

The Layout Platform defines the organizational structure.

The Docking Platform governs operator interaction with that structure.

6. Relationship with the Panel Platform

Every dock node references an existing runtime panel.

Dependency hierarchy:

Dock Node
        │
        ▼
Panel
        │
        ▼
Capability
        │
        ▼
Provider
