STAGE-25 → PHASE-09 → STEP-03
Service State

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-03 establishes the Service State Platform, the institutional subsystem responsible for defining the canonical operational state model of every Service within the COREI Operating System.

While the Service Lifecycle defines how a Service progresses through its operational lifecycle, the Service State defines the current operational condition of that Service.

The Service State introduces standardized state contracts while remaining independent of runtime execution, orchestration, monitoring, dependency management, and business logic.

2. Architectural Position
COREI Operating System
        │
        ▼
Service Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Composition
        ├── Runtime
        ├── Provider Integration
        ├── Capability Integration
        ├── Application Integration
        ├── Runtime Graph
        └── Runtime Orchestrator

The State Platform becomes the third institutional layer of the Service Platform.

3. Purpose

The Service State Platform answers the institutional question:

"What is the current operational condition of this Service?"

It establishes:

Service Status
Service Health
Service Readiness
Service Availability
Service State

The Service State Platform is not responsible for:

runtime execution
lifecycle sequencing
dependency initialization
provider communication
capability execution
application execution
orchestration
business logic

Those responsibilities remain delegated to their respective institutional platforms.

4. Service State Components

The State Platform introduces five institutional components.

Service Availability

Represents whether a Service is currently available for consumption.

Possible values:

AVAILABLE

UNAVAILABLE

Availability answers:

Can this Service currently accept requests?

Service Health

Represents the operational health of the Service.

Possible values:

HEALTHY

DEGRADED

UNHEALTHY

Health answers:

Is this Service operating correctly?

Service Readiness

Represents whether the Service is ready to perform work.

Possible values:

READY

NOT_READY

Readiness answers:

Can the Service safely begin processing?

Service Status

Represents the current lifecycle status of the Service.

Possible values:

INITIALIZING

STARTING

RUNNING

STOPPING

STOPPED

FAILED

Status answers:

Which operational phase is the Service currently in?

Service State

The Service State aggregates every operational state indicator into a single authoritative model.

Each Service State contains:

Status
Health
Readiness
Availability

This becomes the canonical operational state contract for every COREI Service.

5. State Model

The institutional Service State is represented as:

Service State
      │
      ├── Status
      ├── Health
      ├── Readiness
      └── Availability

This structure provides a complete operational view while maintaining strict separation between state representation and runtime behavior.

6. Relationship with the Lifecycle Platform

The Lifecycle defines operational progression.

The State reflects the current condition within that progression.

Relationship:

Service Lifecycle
        │
        ▼
Service State

Lifecycle answers:

What stage should the Service be in?

State answers:

What condition is the Service currently in?

7. Relationship with the Runtime Platform

The Runtime executes the Service.

The State reports the outcome of that execution.

Relationship:

Service Runtime
        │
        ▼
Service State

Runtime performs execution.

State represents operational condition.

8. Relationship with the Runtime Orchestrator

The Runtime Orchestrator will later coordinate execution.

The State provides operational visibility during orchestration.

Relationship:

Runtime Orchestrator
        │
        ▼
Service State

The Orchestrator manages execution.

The State exposes execution status.

9. Repository Organization

The State subsystem has been organized under:

src/services/state/

Current repository structure:

index.ts

service-availability.ts

service-health.ts

service-readiness.ts

service-state.ts

service-status.ts

Each file follows the COREI institutional principle of single responsibility.

10. Architectural Separation

The State Platform owns:

availability model
health model
readiness model
status model
state aggregation
state exports

It does not own:

runtime implementation
lifecycle sequencing
orchestration
monitoring
dependency resolution
business logic

These responsibilities remain isolated within their respective platforms.

11. Dependency Rules

The State Platform follows the approved COREI dependency hierarchy.

Allowed:

Service Runtime
        │
        ▼
Service State

Allowed:

Service Lifecycle
        │
        ▼
Service State

Allowed:

Runtime Orchestrator
        │
        ▼
Service State

Forbidden:

Service State
        │
        ▼
Business Logic

Forbidden:

Service State
        │
        ▼
Provider Runtime

Forbidden:

Service State
        │
        ▼
Capability Runtime

The State Platform remains purely declarative.

12. Practical State Examples
Example 1 — Healthy Service
Status        : RUNNING

Health        : HEALTHY

Readiness     : READY

Availability  : AVAILABLE

Meaning:

Service is running normally.
Requests are accepted.
Dependencies are operational.
No degradation exists.
Example 2 — Starting Service
Status        : STARTING

Health        : HEALTHY

Readiness     : NOT_READY

Availability  : UNAVAILABLE

Meaning:

Startup is still in progress.
Requests should not yet be accepted.
Example 3 — Degraded Service
Status        : RUNNING

Health        : DEGRADED

Readiness     : READY

Availability  : AVAILABLE

Meaning:

Service remains operational.
Performance or dependencies are degraded.
Requests can still be processed.
Example 4 — Failed Service
Status        : FAILED

Health        : UNHEALTHY

Readiness     : NOT_READY

Availability  : UNAVAILABLE

Meaning:

Service has failed.
Runtime has stopped.
No requests should be accepted.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-09 → STEP-04
Service Composition

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-04 establishes the Service Composition Platform, the institutional subsystem responsible for defining how every Service is structurally assembled within the COREI Operating System.

While the Service State defines the operational condition of a Service, the Service Composition defines the internal structural organization of that Service.

The Service Composition introduces standardized composition contracts while remaining independent of runtime execution, orchestration, dependency resolution, monitoring, and business logic.

2. Architectural Position
COREI Operating System
        │
        ▼
Service Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Composition
        ├── Runtime
        ├── Provider Integration
        ├── Capability Integration
        ├── Application Integration
        ├── Runtime Graph
        └── Runtime Orchestrator

The Composition Platform becomes the fourth institutional layer of the Service Platform.

3. Purpose

The Service Composition Platform answers the institutional question:

"How is a Service structurally organized?"

It establishes:

Service Area
Service Region
Service Component Binding
Service Composition
Composition Public API

The Service Composition Platform is not responsible for:

runtime execution
lifecycle sequencing
state management
dependency initialization
provider interaction
capability execution
orchestration
business logic

Those responsibilities remain delegated to their respective institutional platforms.

4. Service Composition Components

The Composition Platform introduces four institutional components.

Service Area

Represents the logical classification of a Service region.

Possible values:

CORE

DOMAIN

INFRASTRUCTURE

The Area categorizes the purpose of each Service region.

Service Region

Represents a structural region within a Service.

Each Region defines:

Region Identifier
Region Name
Area Classification

Examples:

Core Region

Infrastructure Region

Domain Region

Regions provide structural organization only.

Service Component Binding

Defines how internal components belong to Service Regions.

Each binding specifies:

Component Identifier
Region Identifier

Bindings describe composition relationships without introducing runtime behavior.

Service Composition

Represents the complete structural organization of a Service.

Each composition contains:

Regions
Component Bindings

The Composition becomes the authoritative structural model for every COREI Service.

5. Composition Model

The institutional Service Composition is represented as:

Service Composition
        │
        ├── Regions
        │
        └── Component Bindings

This structure provides complete architectural organization while remaining independent of execution.

6. Relationship with the State Platform

The State Platform defines operational condition.

The Composition Platform defines structural organization.

Relationship:

Service State
        │
        ▼
Service Composition

State answers:

What condition is the Service in?

Composition answers:

How is the Service organized?

7. Relationship with the Runtime Platform

The Runtime executes the Service.

The Composition describes what is executed.

Relationship:

Service Composition
        │
        ▼
Service Runtime

Composition provides structure.

Runtime performs execution.

8. Relationship with Future Integrations

Future integration layers operate on the Service Composition.

Provider Integration
        │
Capability Integration
        │
Application Integration
        │
        ▼
Service Composition

Composition becomes the structural foundation upon which integrations are attached.

The Composition subsystem has been organized under:

src/services/composition/

Current repository structure:

service-composition.ts

service-region.ts

Each file follows the COREI institutional principle of single responsibility.

10. Architectural Separation

The Composition Platform owns:

structural organization
Service regions
component bindings
composition model
composition exports

It does not own:

runtime execution
lifecycle management
state transitions
dependency initialization
orchestration
business logic

These responsibilities remain isolated within their respective institutional platforms.

11. Dependency Rules

The Composition Platform follows the approved COREI dependency hierarchy.

Allowed:

Service Composition
        │
        ▼
Service Runtime

Allowed:

Provider Integration
        │
        ▼
Service Composition

Allowed:

Capability Integration
        │
        ▼
Service Composition

Forbidden:

Service Composition
        │
        ▼
Runtime Execution

Forbidden:

Service Composition
        │
        ▼
Business Logic

Forbidden:

Service Composition
        │
        ▼
Provider Runtime

The Composition Platform remains purely declarative.

12. Practical Composition Example

Example Service:

Market Data Service

Composition:

Market Data Service
        │
        ├── Core Region
        │      ├── Subscription Manager
        │      └── Session Manager
        │
        ├── Domain Region
        │      ├── Feed Processor
        │      └── Symbol Resolver
        │
        └── Infrastructure Region
               ├── Logger
               ├── Metrics
               └── Cache Adapter

Component bindings associate each internal component with its designated region while remaining independent of runtime execution.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-09 → STEP-05
Service Runtime

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-05 establishes the Service Runtime Platform, the institutional subsystem responsible for defining the executable runtime contract of every Service within the COREI Operating System.

While the Service Composition defines how a Service is structurally organized, the Service Runtime defines how that Service executes throughout its operational lifetime.

The Service Runtime introduces standardized execution contracts while remaining independent of orchestration, dependency topology, provider initialization, capability execution, and business logic.

2. Architectural Position
COREI Operating System
        │
        ▼
Service Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Composition
        ├── Runtime
        ├── Provider Integration
        ├── Capability Integration
        ├── Application Integration
        ├── Runtime Graph
        └── Runtime Orchestrator

The Runtime Platform becomes the fifth institutional layer of the Service Platform.

3. Purpose

The Service Runtime Platform answers the institutional question:

"How does a Service execute?"

It establishes:

executable runtime contract
runtime operations
default runtime implementation
runtime public API

The Service Runtime Platform is not responsible for:

dependency sequencing
runtime orchestration
provider initialization
capability initialization
application coordination
runtime topology
business logic

Those responsibilities remain delegated to later institutional layers.

4. Service Runtime Components

The Runtime Platform introduces two institutional components.

Service Runtime

Defines the canonical executable runtime contract for every COREI Service.

The Runtime exposes seven deterministic operations:

initialize()

load()

activate()

suspend()

resume()

unload()

shutdown()

These operations define the executable interface only.

No orchestration or dependency sequencing occurs within the Runtime.

Default Service Runtime

Provides the institutional default implementation of the runtime contract.

At this stage, each operation provides an empty implementation.

Future Service implementations will extend this runtime while preserving the standardized execution contract.

5. Runtime Execution Model

Every Service exposes the same deterministic runtime progression.

initialize()
        │
        ▼
load()
        │
        ▼
activate()
        │
        ▼
suspend()
        │
        ▼
resume()
        │
        ▼
unload()
        │
        ▼
shutdown()

This progression defines executable operations only.

Execution ordering will later be coordinated by the Runtime Orchestrator.

6. Relationship with the Lifecycle Platform

The Lifecycle defines operational stages.

The Runtime implements executable behavior for those stages.

Relationship:

Service Lifecycle
        │
        ▼
Service Runtime

Lifecycle answers:

Which operational stage exists?

Runtime answers:

How is that stage executed?

7. Relationship with the Runtime Graph

The Runtime Graph will later define dependency topology.

Relationship:

Service Runtime Graph
        │
        ▼
Service Runtime

The Runtime Graph answers:

What dependencies exist?

The Runtime answers:

How does the Service execute?

The Runtime remains independent of dependency modeling.

8. Relationship with the Runtime Orchestrator

The Runtime Orchestrator will later coordinate runtime execution.

Relationship:

Runtime Orchestrator
        │
        ▼
Service Runtime

The Runtime exposes executable operations.

The Orchestrator determines execution order.

9. Repository Organization

The Runtime subsystem has been organized under:

src/services/runtime/

Current repository structure:

index.ts

service-runtime.ts

Each file follows the COREI institutional principle of single responsibility.

10. Architectural Separation

The Runtime Platform owns:

runtime contract
executable operations
runtime abstraction
default runtime implementation
runtime exports

It does not own:

orchestration
runtime graph
dependency sequencing
provider execution
capability execution
application coordination
business logic

These responsibilities remain isolated within their respective institutional platforms.

11. Dependency Rules

The Runtime Platform follows the approved COREI dependency hierarchy.

Allowed:

Service Lifecycle
        │
        ▼
Service Runtime

Allowed:

Runtime Orchestrator
        │
        ▼
Service Runtime

Allowed:

Runtime Graph
        │
        ▼
Service Runtime

Forbidden:

Service Runtime
        │
        ▼
Provider Initialization

Forbidden:

Service Runtime
        │
        ▼
Capability Initialization

Forbidden:

Service Runtime
        │
        ▼
Business Logic

The Runtime Platform remains purely an execution contract.

↓

load()

↓

activate()

↓

...running...

↓

↓

resume()

↓

unload()

↓

shutdown()

↓

↓

↓

shutdown()

Every COREI Service follows the identical runtime contract regardless of business responsibility.

STAGE-25 → PHASE-09 → STEP-06
Service Provider Integration

Status: COMPLETED

1. Objective

STEP-06 establishes the Service Provider Integration Platform, the institutional subsystem responsible for defining how every Service declares its infrastructure Provider dependencies within the COREI Operating System.

While the Service Runtime defines how a Service executes, the Service Provider Integration Platform defines which infrastructure Providers a Service depends upon.

The integration layer is purely declarative. It introduces standardized Provider dependency contracts while remaining independent of runtime execution, dependency resolution, orchestration, monitoring, and business logic.

2. Architectural Position
COREI Operating System
        │
        ▼
Service Platform
        │
        ├── Foundation
        ├── Lifecycle
        ├── State
        ├── Composition
        ├── Runtime
        ├── Provider Integration
        ├── Capability Integration
        ├── Application Integration
        ├── Runtime Graph
        └── Runtime Orchestrator

The Provider Integration Platform becomes the sixth institutional layer of the Service Platform.

3. Purpose

The Service Provider Integration Platform answers the institutional question:

"Which infrastructure Providers does this Service require?"

It establishes:

Service Provider contract
Service Provider collection
Provider Integration Public API

The Service Provider Integration Platform is not responsible for:

Provider initialization
Provider runtime execution
dependency resolution
runtime orchestration
lifecycle execution
monitoring
business logic

Those responsibilities remain delegated to the Provider Platform and the Runtime Orchestrator.

4. Service Provider Integration Components

The Provider Integration Platform introduces two institutional components.

Service Provider

Represents a Provider dependency required by a Service.

Each Provider reference defines:

Provider Identifier
Provider Name

The contract establishes the canonical representation of Provider dependencies across the COREI Service Platform.

Service Providers

Represents the complete collection of Provider dependencies required by a Service.

The collection becomes the authoritative declaration of infrastructure dependencies for that Service.

Example:

Market Data Service

Providers

• PostgreSQL Provider

• Redis Provider

• Kafka Provider

• Vault Provider

The collection contains declarations only.

It does not instantiate or execute Providers.

5. Integration Model

The institutional Provider Integration model is represented as:

Service
     │
     ▼
Provider Dependencies
     │
     ├── PostgreSQL
     ├── Redis
     ├── Kafka
     └── Vault

The model defines dependency relationships without performing dependency resolution.

6. Relationship with the Provider Platform

The Provider Platform owns Provider implementation.

The Service Platform declares Provider consumption.

Relationship:

Provider Platform
        │
        ▼
Service Provider Integration

Provider Platform answers:

What infrastructure resources exist?

Provider Integration answers:

Which infrastructure resources does this Service consume?

7. Relationship with the Runtime Platform

The Runtime executes the Service.

The Provider Integration Platform identifies the infrastructure required by that Runtime.

Relationship:

Service Provider Integration
        │
        ▼
Service Runtime

The Runtime consumes declared dependencies.

The integration layer does not perform execution.

8. Relationship with the Runtime Graph

The Runtime Graph will later consolidate Provider dependencies with other Service relationships.

Relationship:

Provider Integration
        │
        ▼
Runtime Graph

The Runtime Graph becomes the authoritative dependency topology by incorporating Provider declarations.

It does not own:

Provider runtime
dependency resolution
runtime execution
orchestration
business logic

Node Name

Service Runtime Graph

Runtime Nodes
Runtime Edges

While the Service Runtime Graph defines the dependency topology, the Service Runtime Orchestrator defines how that topology is executed in a deterministic manner.

The Runtime Orchestrator coordinates execution while remaining independent of business logic, dependency discovery, runtime implementation, monitoring, and lifecycle definition.

This is the final institutional layer of the Service Platform.

Service Runtime Graph

unload execution

expose the Runtime Graph

For example:

Stage-25

Foundation Block
Phase-01 ... Phase-15 (or however many foundational phases are needed)

Enablement Block
Phase-16 Institutional Shell
Phase-17 Capability Provider Integration
Phase-18 Window & Workspace Platform
...

This gives you:

a single, immutable phase numbering sequence,
no duplicate phase identifiers,
complete traceability,
clean repository governance,
and a deterministic execution history.

From an institutional engineering and audit perspective, this is the approach I would adopt. It preserves every implementation artifact already created while keeping the overall Stage-25 roadmap coherent and scalable.

This is exactly the right question to ask before we continue. We are at an architectural checkpoint, and if we make the wrong decision now, it will affect hundreds of future files.

After reviewing both execution models, my recommendation is not to jump back to the old Phase-03 after Phase-13. There are still foundational platforms missing.

First Principle

What we are currently building is the COREI Platform Kernel.

Everything in the original roadmap (Shell, Providers, Trading, AI, etc.) depends on this kernel.

So the question becomes:

Is the kernel complete after Domain Platform?

My answer is: No.

1. Remaining Foundation Phases

Based on everything we have designed for COREI, I would consider the foundation complete only after the following platforms exist.

Current Phase	Platform	Status
Phase-01	Provider Platform	✓
Phase-02	Capability Platform	✓
Phase-03	Cognitive Platform	✓
Phase-04	Workspace Foundation	✓
Phase-05	Workspace Runtime	✓
Phase-06	Workspace Infrastructure	✓
Phase-07	Experience Platform	✓
Phase-08	Application Platform	✓
Phase-09	Service Platform	✓
Phase-10	Module Platform	Next
Phase-11	Engine Platform	Pending
Phase-12	Domain Platform	Pending
Phase-13	Platform Runtime Integration	Pending
Phase-14	Event Platform	Missing
Phase-15	State Platform	Missing
Phase-16	Extension Platform	Missing

These last three are not arbitrary additions. They are already implied by your architecture:

Event Bus
Global State
Plugin/Extension system

Today they are scattered across documents, but they deserve first-class ownership.

After Phase-16, I would consider the COREI Platform Kernel complete.

Why these three?
Event Platform

Everything in COREI is event-driven.

Examples:

Trading events
UI events
Runtime events
Provider events
AI events
Infrastructure events

Today every platform mentions events, but no platform owns them.

That is an architectural gap.

State Platform

Almost every platform has its own state.

Examples:

Workspace State
Experience State
Provider State
Service State
Runtime State

But we don't yet have a global institutional state architecture.

Without this:

synchronization
persistence
replay
audit
recovery

become inconsistent.

Extension Platform

This is another missing ownership.

Later you'll add:

plugins
AI extensions
broker adapters
exchange adapters
research modules
custom widgets
automation packages

Who owns extension loading?

Who validates extensions?

Who versions them?

Who unloads them?

Today: nobody.

Institutionally, this should be its own platform.

Therefore

The kernel becomes:

Phase-01  Provider Platform
Phase-02  Capability Platform
Phase-03  Cognitive Platform
Phase-04  Workspace Foundation
Phase-05  Workspace Runtime
Phase-06  Workspace Infrastructure
Phase-07  Experience Platform
Phase-08  Application Platform
Phase-09  Service Platform
Phase-10  Module Platform
Phase-11  Engine Platform
Phase-12  Domain Platform
Phase-13  Platform Runtime Integration
Phase-14  Event Platform
Phase-15  State Platform
Phase-16  Extension Platform

At this point the institutional kernel is complete.

2. Where should the old roadmap continue?

Not from old Phase-03.

The old roadmap should begin after the kernel.

That means:

New Phase	Original Phase
Phase-17	Platform Bootstrap
Phase-18	Platform Foundation
Phase-19	Institutional Shell
Phase-20	Capability Provider Integration
Phase-21	Window & Workspace Platform
Phase-22	Panel & Visualization Platform
Phase-23	Operator Productivity Platform
Phase-24	Workspace Intelligence
Phase-25	Identity & Organization Platform
Phase-26	Governance Platform
Phase-27	Institutional Scale Platform
Phase-28	Infrastructure Integration Platform
Phase-29	Trading Capability Platform
Phase-30	Experience Implementation
Phase-31	Intelligence Platform
Phase-32	Performance Platform
Phase-33	Production Readiness
Phase-34	Institutional Validation & Freeze
Why not continue from old Phase-03?

Because the old Phase-01 and Phase-02 describe things like:

Bootstrap
Configuration
Registry
Service Framework
State Framework
Event Framework

Those are already being built inside the kernel.

If we start those phases later, we will duplicate work and create ownership conflicts.

My recommendation

I would make one adjustment to your original roadmap.

Keep:

Institutional Shell
Capability Provider Integration
Window & Workspace Platform
Panel & Visualization Platform
Operator Productivity Platform
Workspace Intelligence
Identity & Organization
Governance
Institutional Scale
Infrastructure Integration
Trading Capability
Experience
Intelligence
Performance
Production
Validation

But retire the original "Platform Bootstrap" and "Platform Foundation" phases as standalone implementation phases. Their responsibilities have already been absorbed into the kernel platforms we've been building.

Final Professional Roadmap

This gives you a clean two-stage execution:

Stage A — Platform Kernel (Phases 01–16)

Build every reusable platform with clear ownership:

Provider
Capability
Cognitive
Workspace
Experience

I believe this is the cleanest institutional solution because it preserves all completed work, avoids renumbering, eliminates duplicated responsibilities, and provides a clear separation between building the operating system kernel and building the operational capabilities that run on that kernel.

platform events

trading events
infrastructure events
UI events
AI events

Without this platform event ownership becomes fragmented.

State Platform

Purpose

Provide institutional ownership for:

global state
synchronization
persistence
replay
recovery
audit state

Every subsystem already contains local state.

The State Platform becomes the institutional layer coordinating all state management.

Extension Platform

Purpose

Provide institutional ownership for:

plugins
broker adapters
exchange adapters
AI extensions
automation packages
custom widgets
future marketplace packages

Without this platform extension ownership becomes scattered across the repository.

The numbering continues sequentially.

No phase numbers are reused.

9. Why Original Phase-01 and Phase-02 Are Not Reused

The original roadmap began with:

Platform Bootstrap

Platform Foundation

During implementation those responsibilities were absorbed into the Platform Kernel.

Examples:

configuration
registry
service framework
runtime
lifecycle
state

diagnostics

These are now distributed across the institutional kernel platforms.

Therefore implementing them again would duplicate ownership and create architectural conflicts.

For this reason the original Platform Bootstrap and Platform Foundation phases are considered superseded by the Platform Kernel and are not reintroduced as separate implementation phases.

12. Architectural Continuity Rule

For all future implementation work:

Preserve the current Stage-25 phase numbering.
Do not renumber completed phases or modify existing file headers.
Complete the remaining Platform Kernel phases (10–16) before beginning Platform Enablement.
Continue phase numbering sequentially into the Enablement block (17–32), avoiding duplicate or reused phase numbers.
Maintain deterministic platform symmetry: each foundational platform follows the same institutional lifecycle (Foundation → Lifecycle → State → Composition → Runtime → Integrations → Runtime Graph → Runtime Orchestrator, or the approved variant for that platform).

This continuity plan preserves repository integrity, implementation traceability, and the long-term institutional architecture while allowing the project to continue without breaking existing code, documentation, or governance.

I have reviewed this continuity document carefully, and it is internally consistent with the institutional architecture you've been building. It resolves the continuity problem that naturally emerged as Stage-25 evolved from a business roadmap into an operating-system kernel implementation.

Assessment

This document establishes several architectural governance decisions that should now be treated as frozen.

1. Phase numbering is immutable

This is the correct decision.

Once implementation files, validation reports, repository history, and documentation reference phase numbers, renumbering them would break:

implementation traceability
audit history
source code metadata
documentation lineage
repository consistency

Institutionally, freezing completed phase numbers is the correct governance model.

2. Separation into Kernel and Enablement blocks

This is a much cleaner execution model.

Instead of mixing infrastructure with business functionality, Stage-25 now becomes:

Stage-25

Block-A
COREI Platform Kernel

↓

Block-B
COREI Platform Enablement

This matches how mature operating systems evolve.

First build reusable runtime infrastructure.

Then build user-facing functionality on top.

3. Kernel-first architecture

The reasoning is technically sound.

For example:

Trading Experience

↓

Workspace

↓

Capabilities

↓

Providers

↓

Foundation

cannot exist unless every lower layer already exists.

The same applies to:

runtime
lifecycle
state
events
dependency resolution
orchestration

These are kernel responsibilities rather than business responsibilities.

4. Missing kernel platforms

The addition of the remaining kernel platforms is architecturally justified.

Module Platform

Owns modular functional units.

Engine Platform

Owns execution engines.

Domain Platform

Owns institutional business domains.

Platform Runtime Integration

Owns cross-platform runtime coordination.

Event Platform

Owns institutional event governance.

State Platform

Owns global state governance beyond local platform state.

Extension Platform

Owns extensibility and plugin architecture.

These are all cross-cutting concerns that should exist as first-class platforms rather than being scattered across the repository.

5. Original roadmap preservation

A particularly strong aspect is that the original roadmap is not discarded.

Instead:

it is deferred,
renumbering is avoided,
repository continuity is preserved.

That prevents architectural churn.

Architectural dependency

The resulting dependency chain becomes:

Foundation

↓

Provider Platform

↓

Capability Platform

↓

Cognitive Platform

↓

Workspace Platform

↓

Experience Platform

↓

Application Platform

↓

Service Platform

↓

Module Platform

↓

Engine Platform

↓

Domain Platform

↓

Platform Runtime Integration

↓

Event Platform

↓

State Platform

↓

Extension Platform

============================

Kernel Complete

============================

Institutional Shell

↓

Capability Provider Integration

↓

Window Platform

↓

Visualization

↓

Trading

↓

Production

This is logically progressive and preserves strict dependency direction.

One recommendation

I recommend making one governance rule explicit and treating it as a permanent architectural law:

No platform in Block-B may introduce foundational runtime infrastructure that properly belongs in Block-A.

In practical terms:

If, during Phase-21, you discover a generic runtime service that every platform would need, do not implement it inside Phase-21.
Instead, classify it as a missing kernel capability and evaluate whether it belongs in one of the existing kernel platforms (or, only if absolutely necessary, a newly approved kernel platform before kernel freeze).

This prevents higher-level business phases from accumulating hidden infrastructure and preserves the clean separation between the operating-system kernel and platform enablement.

Execution baseline

From this point forward, I will treat this continuity document as the authoritative execution model for Stage-25.

The implementation sequence is therefore:

BLOCK-A — COREI Platform Kernel

✓ Phase-02  Capability Platform
✓ Phase-03  Cognitive Platform
✓ Phase-04  Workspace Foundation
✓ Phase-05  Workspace Runtime

✓ Phase-09  Service Platform

→ Phase-10  Module Platform
→ Phase-11  Engine Platform
→ Phase-12  Domain Platform
→ Phase-13  Platform Runtime Integration
→ Phase-14  Event Platform
→ Phase-15  State Platform
→ Phase-16  Extension Platform

BLOCK-B — COREI Platform Enablement

→ Phase-17 through Phase-32

I will also preserve the institutional implementation pattern you've established for each platform: build the platform through deterministic governance layers, validate it completely, freeze it, and only then allow higher architectural layers to consume it. This maintains repository integrity, architectural symmetry, and long-term maintainability.
